Mr. Zuckerberg is the founder, Chief Executive Officer, and Chairman of the Board of Directors (the "Board") of the Company. He has held a controlling voting interest in the Company since its inception. As of October 1, 2026, Mr. Zuckerberg 15 beneficially owned 1,220,703 shares of Class A common stock and 341,215,148 shares of Class B common stock, collectively representing approximately 60.6% of the Company’s total outstanding voting power and approximately 13.4% of its total outstanding economic interests. As of March 31, 2013, within a year of the Company’s initial public offering, Mr. Zuckerberg’s voting interest in the Company represented approximately 53.7% and has increased approximately 6.9 percentage points in the last thirteen years due, in part, to the Company’s repurchase of Class A common stock over the years and sales of Class B common stock by other holders of such stock. The Board has recognized that a significant portion of the Company’s success has been attributable to Mr. Zuckerberg’s leadership, creative vision, and management abilities, and that Mr. Zuckerberg’s continued leadership role in the Company should be expected to provide substantial benefits to the Company and its shareholders. From time to time, the Board has considered the Company’s optimal governance structure and capital structure in order to, among other things, incentivize Mr. Zuckerberg, to facilitate his effective leadership, and to maximize value for all of the Company’s shareholders. In late 2025, the Board sought to evaluate whether the Company had the appropriate capital structure in place to facilitate the Company’s next growth cycle and long-term plans in the face of its rapidly evolving industry and the AI boom. In particular, the Board was focused on ensuring that the Company’s capital structure would enable creative and flexible approaches to capital allocation and financing and would permit the Company to take advantage of opportunities when they arise, while balancing the need to incentivize Mr. Zuckerberg to continue to guide the Company’s long-term strategy. Because any comprehensive review of the Company’s capital structure and financing needs would require consideration of various potential options, such as the issuance of additional equity or the creation of a new class of stock, that could have implications for Mr. Zuckerberg’s voting control, the Board viewed the creation of a Special Committee as necessary to conduct such a review. On February 12, 2026, the Compensation, Nominating & Governance Committee of the Board (“CN&G Committee”) recommended that the Board form a special committee composed solely of independent and disinterested directors to evaluate potential modifications to the capital structure of the Company, including the ability to trade Class B common stock and the potential creation of a new class of low- or no-vote shares (a “Potential Recapitalization”). Later that same day, following a review of the disinterestedness and independence standards, at the recommendation of the CN&G Committee, the Board established a special committee of independent and disinterested directors (the “Special Committee”) and appointed Ambassador Robert M. Kimmitt, Charles Songhurst, and Tony Xu as its members. The Board noted that, based on its due diligence and analysis, including inquiries regarding each such person, each of the Special Committee members was disinterested and satisfied the applicable criteria for determining director independence from the Company and Mr. Zuckerberg under the rules (and interpretations thereof) promulgated by the Nasdaq Stock Market LLC and under Delaware law. The resolutions creating the Special Committee empowered it to review, consider and make a recommendation regarding potential modifications to the capital structure of the Company, including empowering it to reject any such transaction, and provided that the Board would not approve any such transaction without a prior favorable recommendation of the Special Committee. On February 13, 2026, at the Special Committee’s request, representatives of the Company began identifying potential independent legal and financial advisors to assist the Special Committee in its review of a Potential Recapitalization. Over the course of February and March 2026, the Special Committee solicited proposals from, and interviewed, multiple potential legal and financial advisors. The Special Committee ultimately determined to retain Skadden, Arps, Slate, Meagher & Flom LLP ("Special Committee Counsel") as its independent legal advisor based on its expertise, experience, and independence from Mr. Zuckerberg. In assessing Special Committee Counsel’s independence, the Special Committee was aware of, and took into account, that Special Committee Counsel: (i) had represented Chan Zuckerberg Initiative, LLC ("CZI"), an entity controlled by Mr. Zuckerberg, in one strategic transaction in November 2025, for which it was paid $4 million (representing less than 0.2% of its 2025 revenue); and (ii) had not otherwise been engaged by CZI. The Special Committee also noted that Special Committee Counsel had never represented the Company or Mr. Zuckerberg and had represented other clients in multiple matters adverse to the Company, including in litigation. Pending its formal retention by the Special Committee, Special Committee Counsel then began to assist the Special Committee in assessing the independence and disinterestedness of each potential financial advisor candidate. During that same period, Special Committee Counsel also conducted its own assessment of the independence and disinterestedness of each of the members of the Special Committee. Special Committee Counsel concluded that each of the members of the Special Committee is independent from Mr. Zuckerberg and every other Board member and had no material interest in any Potential Recapitalization. On March 16, 2026, Moelis & Company LLC ("Moelis") executed a non-disclosure agreement with the Company. On March 18, 2026, Mr. Zuckerberg’s legal counsel ("Founder Counsel") 1 reached out to Special Committee Counsel stating that they understood the Special Committee had retained Special Committee Counsel and to open a line of communication with respect to a Potential Recapitalization. 1 Founder Counsel were, at this time, partners of Wachtell, Lipton, Rosen & Katz who, during the course of the events described herein, later became partners of Gibson, Dunn & Crutcher LLP. The term “Founder Counsel” includes these partners and the full team at Gibson, Dunn & Crutcher LLP. 16 On March 23, 2026, at the invitation of the Special Committee, representatives of Moelis pitched to the Special Committee, which pitch included, among other topics, Moelis’ experience advising in recapitalizations involving controlled companies, key questions and considerations potentially relevant for the Special Committee in connection with its assessment of a Potential Recapitalization and the range of structural and governance alternatives available based on precedent transactions of a similar nature. On March 26, 2026, at the request of the Company’s management, Special Committee Counsel had an organizational meeting with members of the Company’s management to discuss the Special Committee’s overall process in considering a Potential Recapitalization and anticipated next steps. On March 27, 2026, the Board expanded and formalized the Special Committee’s authority, before the Special Committee started any work assessing any Potential Recapitalization, delegating to it the full power and authority of the Board, to the fullest extent permitted under the DGCL, to review, consider, evaluate, and approve or reject any Potential Recapitalization, including the authority to authorize and approve potential financing opportunities for the Company in connection with the Potential Recapitalization. Any actions that could not be delegated to the Special Committee under the DGCL would remain within the purview of the Board. In light of the additional work undertaken by Special Committee Counsel to assess the independence and disinterestedness of the members of the Special Committee, the Board again determined that each member of the Special Committee was independent and disinterested, had no material interest in the Potential Recapitalization, and had no material relationship with any person that could be deemed to have such a material interest in the Potential Recapitalization, including Mr. Zuckerberg, or who lacked independence from Mr. Zuckerberg. Over the course of the next few days, members of the Special Committee held various discussions with Special Committee Counsel regarding the fee proposals and fee structures submitted by the potential financial advisors. During this meeting, Special Committee Counsel updated the Special Committee on the discussion it had with Founder Counsel at the Special Committee’s request, reporting that Mr. Zuckerberg and Founder Counsel had conveyed that Mr. Zuckerberg was prepared to submit a proposal for a Potential Recapitalization in the coming days. Special Committee Counsel also reviewed with the Special Committee its fiduciary duties, the applicable standards of review under Delaware law with respect to the Special Committee’s determination to undertake a Potential Recapitalization, if it should decide to do so, and the Special Committee’s authority and responsibilities with respect to negotiating any such Potential Recapitalization. In addition, Special Committee Counsel noted that Ambassador Kimmitt had disclosed a business relationship with the law firm WilmerHale, which provided approximately $62.3 million of legal services to the Company in 2025 (representing approximately 3.87% of WilmerHale’s annual gross revenue). However, Ambassador Kimmitt is not a partner of WilmerHale, does not receive compensation from WilmerHale tied to the firm’s revenues from the Company, provides no legal services to the Company, and is walled off from the firm’s representation of the Company. Thereafter, the Special Committee and Special Committee Counsel discussed the independence disclosures of and fee proposals from the potential financial advisors, and Special Committee Counsel shared the results of its assessment with the Special Committee. In assessing Moelis' independence, the Special Committee noted that (i) a senior member of the proposed Moelis team, the name of whom was disclosed to the Special Committee, had acquired approximately 30,000 shares of Class A common stock of the Company, with such shares valued at $18,888,391 as of March 17, 2026, through open market purchases after the Company’s initial public offering in May 2012, and that such interest was helpful in confirming that such senior member of the Moelis team was financially aligned with the Class A shareholders and (ii) Moelis disclosed no material relationships with the Company or Mr. Zuckerberg. Following discussion and careful consideration, the Special Committee unanimously ratified the engagement of Special Committee Counsel as its legal counsel and selected Moelis as its financial advisor. The Special Committee selected each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis based upon their respective qualifications; prior experience with recapitalizations of controlled companies, including legal challenges in Delaware; and absence of conflicts with respect to a Potential Recapitalization. The Special Committee also confirmed Ambassador Kimmitt as its chair. On April 13, 2026, Moelis shared a draft engagement letter with Special Committee Counsel. On June 11, 2026, the Special Committee held a meeting with each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis in attendance. During this meeting, representatives of Moelis reviewed the Company’s historical financial performance since its initial public offering, including the performance of certain long-term projects undertaken under Mr. Zuckerberg’s leadership (among them the acquisitions of Instagram and WhatsApp, Reality Labs, and the Company’s significant investments in AI). In addition, representatives of Moelis highlighted the Company’s stock price performance since its initial public offering against the performance of the S&P 500 in the same period, noting that the Company’s stock price had increased significantly more than the S&P 500 in that same period. Moelis also compared the strong growth of the Company’s business relative to its peer companies in the social networking industry. Representatives of Moelis also discussed the current capital raising environment with the Special Committee, including the fact that Alphabet Inc. had recently announced an approximately $80 billion equity capital raise. In light of the Company’s extraordinary performance under Mr. Zuckerberg’s long-term stewardship, the Special Committee acknowledged that the continuation of Mr. Zuckerberg’s control was in the best interests of all shareholders of the Company, including the Class A common shareholders. Representatives of Moelis also shared investor surveys and the voting results of recent shareholder proposals on relevant governance considerations. Representatives of Moelis further discussed with the Special Committee feedback Moelis and Special Committee Counsel had received from management regarding management’s interest in raising additional capital for the Company, illustrative implications as to economic interest and voting control for Mr. Zuckerberg in connection with different equity financing scenarios under the Company’s current capital structure (with Moelis noting that the maximum the Company could raise was approximately $700 billion in equity capital over a number of years without Mr. Zuckerberg’s voting power dropping below 50%), investor feedback regarding the Company’s capital structure and publicized business plans, a broad range of other potential financing alternatives available to the Company, including debt, equity, and equity-linked financing alternatives, some of which would permit the Company to raise capital without diluting Mr. Zuckerberg’s voting power, together with market data on the discounts observed at companies with multiple classes of publicly traded stock and the Company’s 2016 reclassification proposal. Representatives of Moelis also noted that the Company had not yet provided its long-range plan for the Moelis and Special Committee Counsel teams to review, but that the Company’s management had orally conveyed to Special Committee Counsel and Moelis that management had previously presented three forecast cases to the Board during a regularly scheduled Board meeting in May, and that, in each case, these long-term forecasts showed the Company’s need to raise hundreds of billions of dollars over a period of several years beginning as early as fall 2026. The Special Committee also discussed various legal and governance considerations relating to a Potential Recapitalization, including the process required for obtaining any required shareholder approval for a Potential Recapitalization, the overall timeline, and the sequencing of next steps. The Special Committee discussed with Special Committee Counsel and Moelis various governance concessions to sunset Mr. Zuckerberg’s control in certain circumstances that the Special Committee could seek from Mr. Zuckerberg if the Special Committee ultimately determined to approve and recommend a Potential Recapitalization to the Board involving the issuance of non-voting common stock as a dividend to existing shareholders. Special Committee Counsel described the process in 2016, and the terms proposed at the time, where a special committee of the Board negotiated with Mr. Zuckerberg a number of sunset provisions for the Company’s Class B common stock upon the occurrence of certain trigger events. Following discussion, the Special Committee directed Special Committee Counsel to prepare a governance proposal setting forth certain sunset terms for the Company’s Class B common stock to deliver to Mr. Zuckerberg in connection with any authorization of non-voting stock and the distribution of such non-voting stock to holders of the Company’s Class A common stock and Class B common stock. Later that day, and following subsequent discussion regarding proposed governance concessions the Special Committee would seek from Mr. Zuckerberg, the Special Committee authorized Special Committee Counsel to deliver a governance proposal. Such proposal would set forth, among other items, sunset provisions on the Company’s Class B common stock pursuant to which all shares of Class B common stock would convert into Class A common stock within a year of the death or disability of Mr. Zuckerberg, his termination from the Company (with or without cause), and Mr. Zuckerberg 20 ceasing to hold at least 50% of the equity securities in the Company he held as of the time the non-voting common stock was authorized (such governance terms, the "Governance Proposal"). On June 24, 2026, Special Committee Counsel had a call with Founder Counsel to preview the Governance Proposal. Following that discussion, Special Committee Counsel delivered the Governance Proposal to Founder Counsel in writing. On July 2, 2026, Founder Counsel advised Special Committee Counsel that the Governance Proposal would likely be unacceptable to Mr. Zuckerberg because Mr. Zuckerberg did not believe his voting position should be adversely affected by a proposal intended to strengthen the Company and meet its capital needs. Later that same day, Special Committee Counsel met with members of management for a regularly scheduled meeting to discuss the status of the Special Committee’s deliberations regarding a Potential Recapitalization, the status of negotiations with Mr. Zuckerberg and his representatives and additional diligence questions posed by representatives of Moelis. On July 3, 2026, each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis briefed Ambassador Kimmitt on the July 2 conversation with Founder Counsel and discussions with management. During this meeting, Special Committee Counsel and Moelis discussed with the Special Committee, among other things, the initial feedback received from Founder Counsel regarding the Governance Proposal and proposed next steps. Special Committee Counsel reported on its July 2 conversation with Founder Counsel. At the end of this meeting, consensus emerged among the Special Committee that Special Committee Counsel should reconnect with Founder Counsel to emphasize the Special Committee’s expectation to receive Mr. Zuckerberg’s feedback on the Governance Proposal. Following the meeting, Special Committee Counsel contacted Founder Counsel and requested a formal response from Mr. Zuckerberg to the Governance Proposal, together with his views and management’s views on the timing of a Potential Recapitalization. Founder Counsel agreed to revert with a response from Mr. Zuckerberg and on a perspective for timing, acknowledging that given the status of negotiations between the Special Committee and Mr. Zuckerberg, an announcement of a Potential Recapitalization by the end of July was unlikely. During this meeting, Special Committee Counsel noted that, in view of the current status of discussions between Special Committee Counsel and Founder Counsel, it seemed unlikely that the Company would be able to announce a Potential Recapitalization by the end of July and contemporaneously with the Company’s earnings release for the second fiscal quarter. On July 8, 2026, following a request from Mr. Zuckerberg to discuss matters unrelated to a Potential Recapitalization, Ambassador Kimmitt, in his capacity as Lead Independent Director of the Board, had a conversation with Mr. Zuckerberg, in his capacity as Chief Executive Officer of the Company. However, during this conversation, Mr. Zuckerberg brought up a Potential Recapitalization and indicated that he understood it was unlikely that a Potential Recapitalization could be announced before the end of July and that the Special Committee had raised significant governance considerations in its Governance Proposal. Ambassador Kimmitt and Mr. Zuckerberg also briefly discussed California Proposition 40, an initiative on the November 3, 2026 California General Election ballot that would impose a one-time wealth tax (“California Proposition 40”), and the resulting tax liability that may be imposed on Mr. Zuckerberg if it is introduced to law and Mr. Zuckerberg’s ability to pay any such tax. Mr. Zuckerberg noted that he has taken only a $1 annual salary since 2013 for serving as the Company’s Chief Executive Officer, and he stated that if he needed to pay any such tax by selling down his Class B common stock, the tax liability could be large enough to reduce his voting interest in the Company to approximately 53%. On July 13, 2026, Special Committee Counsel reached out to Founder Counsel requesting a status update on Mr. Zuckerberg’s response to the Governance Proposal. On July 15, 2026, the Special Committee held a regularly scheduled meeting with Special Committee Counsel and Moelis in attendance. During this meeting, the parties discussed the latest status of a Potential Recapitalization. Special Committee Counsel reported to the Special Committee that Special Committee Counsel had not received additional substantive feedback or communications from Founder Counsel on the substance of the Governance Proposal since their last conversation with Founder Counsel on July 2. At the meeting, Ambassador Kimmitt also summarized to the Special Committee his conversation with Mr. Zuckerberg on July 8, 2026. Representatives of Moelis also discussed less favorable debt market conditions for hyperscalers and its assessment of potential scenarios in which Mr. Zuckerberg’s voting interest could drop to approximately 53% or lower if California Proposition 40 were to become law and he was ultimately obligated to pay a significant tax. Later that same day, Special Committee Counsel and Moelis had a regularly scheduled call with certain members of the Company’s management. During that call, Special Committee Counsel and Moelis discussed updates with respect to a 21 Potential Recapitalization and noted that they still had not received further feedback from Founder Counsel since the last meeting between the Company’s management and Special Committee Counsel and Moelis. On July 17, 2026, Special Committee Counsel contacted Founder Counsel to again inquire as to the status of Mr. Zuckerberg’s response to the Governance Proposal. Founder Counsel noted that Mr. Zuckerberg had been briefed on the Governance Proposal, acknowledged that a response remained outstanding from Mr. Zuckerberg, and indicated that they would follow up with Mr. Zuckerberg. On July 24, 2026, Founder Counsel reached out to Special Committee Counsel to inquire as to any new developments. Special Committee Counsel responded that the Special Committee expected a formal written response to the Special Committee’s Governance Proposal of June 24. On July 28, 2026, following a request from Mr. Zuckerberg to discuss matters unrelated to a Potential Recapitalization, Ambassador Kimmitt, in his capacity as Lead Independent Director of the Board, had a conversation with Mr. Zuckerberg, in his capacity as Chief Executive Officer of the Company. However, during this conversation, Mr. Zuckerberg inquired as to the status of a Potential Recapitalization, to which Ambassador Kimmitt responded that the Special Committee had submitted the Governance Proposal to Mr. Zuckerberg on June 24, 2026, and was expecting a response from Mr. Zuckerberg. Ambassador Kimmitt and Mr. Zuckerberg then briefly discussed the terms of the Governance Proposal, and Mr. Zuckerberg again raised concerns about California Proposition 40 and how the proposition could impact his ability to remain resident in California and pay any tax assessed on him if it becomes law. On July 29, 2026, the Special Committee held a regularly scheduled meeting with each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis in attendance. During this meeting, Special Committee Counsel provided an update on discussions with Founder Counsel since the last Special Committee meeting. Ambassador Kimmitt reported on his conversation with Mr. Zuckerberg on July 28, 2026. Following Ambassador Kimmitt’s report, the Special Committee affirmed its belief that it was important for Mr. Zuckerberg to continue to lead the Company from California, including because it was crucial that Mr. Zuckerberg remains in close coordination with the Company’s management and in close proximity to Meta Superintelligence Labs. Special Committee Counsel then reviewed with the Special Committee market research on “sunset” provisions for high-vote stock of other controlled companies. On July 30, 2026, each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis held a regularly scheduled meeting with certain members of the Company’s management. During this meeting, members of the Company’s management reiterated their view that the Company had an impending need to raise a significant amount of capital to fund the Company’s long-term business plans and requested the opportunity to present management’s perspective on a Potential Recapitalization to the Special Committee. Also on July 30, 2026, Special Committee Counsel had a conversation with Founder Counsel, who noted that he had spoken with members of the Company’s management to confirm management’s view that an equity raise via a new class of non-voting stock was essential to the Company’s anticipated business plans and anticipated financial needs and that a response to the Governance Proposal would be forthcoming on that basis. On August 3, 2026, Founder Counsel sent Special Committee Counsel a letter responding to the Governance Proposal (the "August 3 Letter"). The August 3 Letter set forth Mr. Zuckerberg’s request that the Special Committee consider two complementary approaches. First, the August 3 Letter requested that the Special Committee consider creating a class of non-voting stock in short order to permit the Company to raise the equity capital needed to fund its strategic plans without diluting Mr. Zuckerberg’s voting position. The August 3 Letter further noted such currency would be familiar to and readily accepted by investors, facilitate investment by non-U.S. investors, and avoid reliance on debt markets. Second, the August 3 Letter requested that the Special Committee discuss with Mr. Zuckerberg a revised compensation arrangement comparable to those recently approved for other founders of a similar profile and importance to their companies, consisting of equity grants tied to rigorous performance benchmarks. The August 3 Letter noted that this would enable Mr. Zuckerberg to address taxes that may be assessed on him if California Proposition 40 were to become law. The August 3 Letter further stated that Mr. Zuckerberg would not consider changes to the terms of his voting shares, including the sunset provisions on the Class B common stock set forth in the Governance Proposal, and that Mr. Zuckerberg was considering establishing residence outside of California in light of the risk that California Proposition 40 could become law. On August 4, 2026, the Special Committee held a regularly scheduled meeting with Special Committee Counsel and Moelis. During the meeting, the Special Committee and its advisors discussed the contents of the August 3 Letter. Representatives of Moelis provided the Special Committee an update on the market’s reaction to the Company’s recent earnings announcement, equity research analyst price targets and commentary, and the recent tightening of credit conditions, particularly for hyperscalers, which was reflected in less demand for the July bond issuance related to the Company's Texas data center joint venture with a financial partner as compared to other issuances. The Special Committee also discussed California Proposition 40, including the uncertainty that it would ultimately be approved and introduced into law. Certain members of the Company’s management joined a portion of the meeting to present management’s perspective on the Company’s critical need to raise equity capital in the near term, its desire to do so with non-voting stock that can also be used 22 for employee equity compensation on a go-forward basis and management’s belief that it is critical that Mr. Zuckerberg continue to lead the Company from its headquarters in California. The Special Committee reiterated to Mr. Zuckerberg that it believed it is in the best interest of the Company and its shareholders for Mr. Zuckerberg to continue leading the Company from California so that he may maintain close coordination with the Company’s management and the Company’s artificial intelligence lab. Later that same day, the Special Committee and Special Committee Counsel held a meeting with Mr. Zuckerberg and Founder Counsel, with C.J. Mahoney, Chief Legal Officer of the Company, also in attendance. During this meeting, Mr. Zuckerberg reiterated his support for the Company’s meeting its financing needs and explained his potential liquidity needs as the basis for his compensation request, and he proposed that the Special Committee address his compensation together with the authorization of Class C common stock to enable the Company’s equity financing. Mr. Zuckerberg also proposed that a dividend of twenty shares of Class C common stock for each share of Class A common stock and Class B common stock, paid to all shareholders, would establish a deep trading market for Class C common stock and give him the flexibility he sought to monetize his economic interest without reducing his voting rights. He stated that he would not agree to the Governance Proposal or support an equity financing that diluted his voting control, and that, in light of California Proposition 40, he intended to establish residence outside California, which he had been advised he would need to do before the November 3, 2026 election. Later that same day, Special Committee Counsel held a regularly scheduled meeting with a member of the Company’s management to further discuss the Potential Recapitalization and consider timing and next steps. On August 5, 2026, the Special Committee held a meeting with Special Committee Counsel and Moelis to discuss the August 3 Letter and the parties’ discussion with Mr. Zuckerberg and Founder Counsel on August 4. During this meeting, the Special Committee engaged in full discussion with Special Committee Counsel and Moelis regarding Mr. Zuckerberg’s positions, including his request for a 20:1 dividend ratio for the issuance of Class C common stock, which the Special Committee viewed as excessive, and the viewpoints of the Company’s management regarding the Company’s need for equity financing and the critical need to keep Mr. Zuckerberg in California to lead the Company at its headquarters. At the conclusion of the meeting, the Special Committee directed Special Committee Counsel to prepare a response to the August 3 Letter for the Special Committee’s review that would address Mr. Zuckerberg’s tax liability, should it arise, the terms of which would be to move forward with (i) the authorization of Class C common stock to facilitate an equity financing and (ii) to provide for a limited dividend for all shareholders that would be payable if, and only if, California Proposition 40 became law and Mr. Zuckerberg became obligated to pay taxes thereunder, in an amount sufficient for him to satisfy such tax obligation. In view of Mr. Zuckerberg’s opposition to sunset provisions relating to the Class B common stock, the Special Committee determined it was not prepared otherwise to recommend the further distribution of the Class C common stock to shareholders of the Company, which would include Mr. Zuckerberg and which would have the effect of permitting Mr. Zuckerberg to sell or otherwise transfer his economic interest in the Company without diluting his voting control. On August 6, 2026, Special Committee Counsel delivered a draft of the response letter to the Special Committee (the "August 6 Letter"). The August 6 Letter set forth the Special Committee’s proposal to authorize Class C common stock to enable the Company to raise equity capital without diluting the voting power of the Company’s shareholders or affecting Mr. Zuckerberg’s majority voting control of the Company, and that a dividend of such common stock would be authorized contemporaneously and payable to all holders of Class A common stock and Class B common stock if, and only if, California Proposition 40 became law and a tax payment by Mr. Zuckerberg became due and payable (the "Contingent Dividend"). The August 6 Letter also provided that the dividend ratio, payable to all shareholders, would be calculated such that the value of the shares of Class C common stock distributed to Mr. Zuckerberg would be in an amount sufficient for him to satisfy such tax. Later that day, at the direction of the Special Committee, Special Committee Counsel sent the August 6 Letter to Founder Counsel. On August 11, 2026, Special Committee Counsel contacted Founder Counsel to inquire about the status of a response to the August 6 Letter. Later that day, Founder Counsel responded that the August 6 Letter was a step in the right direction and that a response would be forthcoming. On August 12, 2026, the Special Committee held a regularly scheduled meeting with each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis in attendance. The Special Committee and its advisors discussed key action items, drafting responsibilities for any required SEC filings and related approval documentation for any Potential Recapitalization and anticipated timing should the Potential Recapitalization proceed. Shortly thereafter, on the same day, Special Committee Counsel received a letter from Founder Counsel responding to the August 6 Letter (the "August 12 Letter"). The August 12 Letter expressed Mr. Zuckerberg’s support of the Special Committee’s proposal to authorize the Class C common stock and his and Founder Counsel’s willingness to work with the Special Committee and its advisors on the mechanics and timing of any issuance and dividend of Class C common stock. The August 12 Letter expressed the view that the Contingent Dividend ignores Mr. Zuckerberg’s nominal salary and reiterated Founder Counsel’s request regarding Mr. Zuckerberg’s compensation for his ongoing leadership of the Company, in light of his 23 nominal annual salary, the compensation program granted to the Company’s other top executives earlier that year (from which Mr. Zuckerberg voluntarily excluded himself), and recent precedent. The August 12 Letter asked for a response from the Special Committee on this issue. Following receipt of the August 12 Letter, the Special Committee reconvened with Special Committee Counsel and Moelis to discuss the substance of the letter. At the meeting, the Special Committee reached a consensus that Mr. Zuckerberg’s request for the Special Committee to consider his compensation should instead be addressed by the CN&G Committee and that the Special Committee would refer the matter to the CN&G Committee. Later that day, Special Committee Counsel had a call with Founder Counsel to preview the Special Committee’s response to the August 12 Letter and to inquire as to anticipated timing of Mr. Zuckerberg’s response following his receipt of an impending response from the Special Committee to the August 12 Letter. On August 13, 2026, at the direction of the Special Committee, Special Committee Counsel sent a letter in response to the August 12 Letter (the "August 13 Letter"), which set forth that the Special Committee intended to move forward with its work to potentially authorize the Class C common stock and the potential issuance of Class C common stock in a capital raise of the Company. The August 13 Letter also noted that the Special Committee had concluded that the CN&G Committee should address Mr. Zuckerberg’s compensation request and that the Special Committee would reach out to the Chair of the CN&G Committee to apprise her of Mr. Zuckerberg’s request. Later that same day, each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis held a regularly scheduled meeting with certain members of the Company’s management to discuss the latest updates with respect to a Potential Recapitalization. During this meeting, Special Committee Counsel informed the Company’s management on the Special Committee’s latest proposal with respect to a Potential Recapitalization and noted that the Special Committee only intended to authorize a dividend of Class C common stock that would be payable if and when California Proposition 40 became law and a tax payment by Mr. Zuckerberg thereunder became due and payable, so that management could plan the timing and mechanics of any financing. Following this conversation, Special Committee Counsel had a subsequent discussion with a member of management who conveyed management’s view that the proposal set forth by the Special Committee in the August 6 Letter and August 13 Letter would not be an attractive option for the Company in respect of its potential equity financing objectives because, without an immediate dividend, the limited liquidity of Class C common stock was likely to result in a discount on the Class C common stock the magnitude of which was uncertain but likely to be significant. On August 17, 2026, the Special Committee held a regularly scheduled meeting with each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis. During this meeting, Special Committee Counsel provided an update on their discussions with members of the Company’s management since the last Special Committee meeting, including that they had conveyed the Special Committee’s willingness to proceed with the authorization of Class C common stock and the Contingent Dividend. In response, the Special Committee’s advisors reported that the Company’s management had indicated that they would likely not pursue an equity financing absent an immediate dividend of Class C common stock to first establish an adequately liquid market for Class C common stock to minimize a resulting discount on Class C common stock in an equity raise, and that Susan Li, the Chief Financial Officer of the Company, requested an opportunity to present management’s views regarding a dividend of Class C common stock to the Special Committee later in the week. During its discussion of potential alternatives at the meeting, the Special Committee and its advisors also discussed the possibility of coupling any sale or transfer by Mr. Zuckerberg of shares of Class C common stock received in any dividend with the sale of a pro rata portion of his Class B common stock. Following the meeting, and at the instruction of the Special Committee, Special Committee Counsel invited Ms. Li to present at the next regularly scheduled meeting of the Special Committee. In addition, Special Committee Counsel had a discussion with Founder Counsel on the liquidity and pricing discount concerns raised by the Company’s management as well as Mr. Zuckerberg’s compensation request and how that related to the Contingent Dividend. Founder Counsel conveyed that Mr. Zuckerberg remained focused on his compensation request and shared management’s concerns about the liquidity and pricing discount. Later that same day, at the request of the Company’s management, Special Committee Counsel had a meeting with members of the Company’s management to discuss recent developments with respect to the potential authorization of Class C common stock. During this meeting, Special Committee Counsel reported on their discussions with Founder Counsel and noted the Special Committee’s view that Mr. Zuckerberg’s compensation request was not within the Special Committee’s authorization from the Board. Special Committee Counsel noted that, while the Special Committee understood the importance of a capital raise to the Company and had previously conveyed a quick timeframe, the new liquidity of Class C common stock and compensation matters raised by Mr. Zuckerberg’s counsel remained unresolved, and that the Special Committee would not be prepared to facilitate a Potential Recapitalization at such time. On August 20, 2026, Special Committee Counsel and Founder Counsel spoke again. Founder Counsel reiterated Mr. Zuckerberg’s compensation request from the August 12 Letter and conveyed Mr. Zuckerberg’s view that his continued control of the Company, and its controlling-shareholder structure, was in the long-term interests of the Company and its shareholders. 24 Special Committee Counsel responded that the Special Committee had addressed the California Proposition 40 tax concern through the Contingent Dividend, that Mr. Zuckerberg’s compensation was a matter for the CN&G Committee, and that the Special Committee was not prepared to authorize a Potential Recapitalization that would permit Mr. Zuckerberg to sell down his economic interest without a corresponding reduction in his voting control. The parties also discussed the possibility of coupling any sale or transfer by Mr. Zuckerberg of shares of Class C common stock with the sale of a pro rata portion of his Class B common stock. Special Committee Counsel asked whether Mr. Zuckerberg was prepared to put forward a proposal to advance the discussions, and Founder Counsel indicated that he would consider the matter. Later that same day, at the request of the Company’s management, each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis held a meeting with members of the Company’s management for a preview of the presentation management planned to give the Special Committee at an upcoming meeting. Members of the Company’s management described the considerations bearing on the size and structure of a potential equity offering, including index inclusion, the discount they would expect in connection with an offering of Class C common stock and their preference to preserve flexibility as to the timing and manner of any issuance. Members of the Company’s management also inquired whether the Special Committee would consider a dividend of Class C common stock in exchange for other terms, and Special Committee Counsel noted that a pro rata sell-down of Class B common stock, as well as sunset provisions, would be constructive from the Special Committee’s perspective. On August 21, 2026, the Special Committee held a meeting with each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis, as well as members of the Company’s management for a portion of the meeting. Before management joined, the Special Committee discussed various structures for a Potential Recapitalization involving a dividend of Class C common stock. Members of the Company’s management then joined the meeting and presented on the Company’s financing objectives, including index inclusion, their preferences on potential offering structures to raise funds for its future capital needs, and their estimate of a discount of approximately five to twenty percent on the issuance of new Class C common stock if the Company were to only proceed with the Contingent Dividend (at the ratio proposed therein). Management indicated that, based on its calculations, Class C common stock issued in the Contingent Dividend would not be immediately eligible for index inclusion, which would likely increase the discount at which the Company would need to issue Class C common stock and potentially the discount at which Class C common stock would trade in relation to Class A common stock thereafter. Following management’s departure from the meeting, the Special Committee instructed Special Committee Counsel to request a proposal on a Potential Recapitalization from Mr. Zuckerberg and directed representatives of Moelis to prepare an analysis of potential alternative transaction structures that could address the Class C common stock liquidity and discount matters raised by the Company’s management. Shortly after the Special Committee meeting, at the request of the Company’s management, each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis held a discussion with members of the Company’s management to debrief on the meeting and consider next steps. Special Committee Counsel reiterated that the Special Committee viewed a coupled, or “stapled,” pro rata sell-down of Class B common stock for each share of Class C common stock sold as a constructive approach because it would preserve the status quo relationship of Mr. Zuckerberg’s voting control to his economic interests following a dividend of Class C common stock. Later that same day, Special Committee Counsel spoke with Founder Counsel and indicated that the Special Committee viewed a stapled pro rata sell-down requirement of Class B common stock upon any sale of Class C common stock as a constructive path forward, as it would preserve the existing relationship between his voting control and his economic interest while simultaneously permitting the Company to raise capital to meet its financing needs. Such a requirement would apply only to Mr. Zuckerberg; no other shareholder’s Class C common stock would be subject to any restriction on sale or transfer. Special Committee Counsel explained the rationale for the approach and conveyed that the Special Committee had asked Mr. Zuckerberg and his counsel to consider such an approach and submit a proposal in response to the August 13 Letter. On August 25, 2026, Special Committee Counsel and Founder Counsel discussed the Special Committee’s request for a proposal. Founder Counsel indicated that they would advise Mr. Zuckerberg to submit a proposal, and that any proposal would likely contemplate the authorization of Class C common stock and a non-contingent pro rata dividend of Class C common stock to shareholders of Class A common stock and Class B common stock. Founder Counsel explained that such a dividend would satisfy the Company’s financing needs, confer no benefit on Mr. Zuckerberg that other shareholders did not share on a pro rata basis, would address the liquidity concern that had prompted his compensation request, so that no change to his compensation arrangement would be needed; by contrast, coupling the dividend with a stapled sell-down of his Class B common stock would afford him no liquidity at all and would subject him to restrictions no other shareholder would bear, leaving his liquidity concern to be addressed, if at all, through a compensation arrangement comparable to those approved for industry leaders of similar stature, the cost of which would be borne by the Company and its shareholders, whereas a pro rata dividend would address that concern at no cost to the Company. Special Committee Counsel responded that a dividend not coupled with a corresponding sale of Class B common stock would permit Mr. Zuckerberg to divorce his economic interest from his voting control, and that the Special Committee’s proposal instead contemplated a dividend sufficient to satisfy Mr. Zuckerberg’s anticipated tax obligation, which also mostly preserved the status quo as to the ratio between his voting control and economic interest. Founder Counsel noted that Mr. Zuckerberg’s compensation was a matter for the Special Committee, 25 and Special Committee Counsel reiterated that it is the Special Committee’s view that Mr. Zuckerberg’s compensation remained a matter for the CN&G Committee. The parties agreed to consider one another’s positions and the alternative structures that might be available. On August 26, 2026, all members of the Special Committee held a meeting with Mr. Zuckerberg, Founder Counsel, Mr. Mahoney and Special Committee Counsel. Mr. Zuckerberg stated that, while the Special Committee’s proposal sought to address the Company’s financing needs, it left unaddressed three closely related matters: the substantial liquidity discount that could attach to Class C common stock following a limited dividend, which would affect the Company and all of its shareholders, his own liquidity (or, alternatively, his compensation), and the maintenance of his voting control. He stated that his compensation and the authorization of Class C common stock were closely related and should be resolved together rather than referred to separate committees and noted that he expected to reach a decision regarding his state of residence within the coming weeks. He also noted that he did not wish to go through additional protracted negotiation processes in the future if taxes similar to California Proposition 40 were proposed in other jurisdictions. The Special Committee conveyed that it was aligned with Mr. Zuckerberg on the authorization of Class C common stock and shared his objective that he continue to lead the Company from California. It noted that the Contingent Dividend, as set forth in the August 6 Letter, had been structured to allow him to satisfy his anticipated tax obligation in full. As to compensation, the Special Committee reiterated that the request had been referred to the CN&G Committee. During the meeting, the Special Committee proposed coupling any sale by Mr. Zuckerberg of Class C common stock with the sale of a pro rata portion of his Class B common stock. Mr. Zuckerberg rejected the proposal and questioned why the preservation of the status quo with respect to the ratio of his voting control to his economic interest in the Company should be a consideration for the Special Committee, citing precedent in other companies where relatively small economic ownership nevertheless conveyed founder voting control. He conveyed his view that his compensation and the authorization of Class C common stock should be resolved together rather than referred to separate committees. The Special Committee asked that Mr. Zuckerberg revert with a proposal, and the parties agreed that the Special Committee would reconvene with its advisors in advance of any further discussion with Mr. Zuckerberg. On August 27, 2026, all members of the Special Committee held a meeting with each of Special Committee Counsel and representatives of Moelis, during which the representatives of Moelis presented a proposal to authorize the Class C common stock and an unqualified dividend (i.e., not conditioned on the enactment of California Proposition 40) to all shareholders with a dividend ratio of one share of Class C common stock for approximately every nine shares of Class A common stock or Class B common stock to all shareholders, not conditioned on the enactment of California Proposition 40, which was intended to address both Mr. Zuckerberg’s liquidity and the Company’s financing objectives without diluting his voting control. The Special Committee and its advisors discussed the proposal and at the conclusion of the meeting, the Special Committee directed Special Committee Counsel to prepare another proposal for the Special Committee’s review on the basis that the Special Committee would authorize an unqualified dividend of one share of Class C common stock for every nine shares of Class A common stock and Class B common stock in order to address Mr. Zuckerberg’s liquidity needs and management’s concerns with respect to the liquidity of Class C common stock and the potential resulting discount on the Class C common stock. On August 28, 2026, Special Committee Counsel had a call with Founder Counsel to preview the Special Committee’s response. Following the discussion, at the direction of the Special Committee, Special Committee Counsel delivered a letter to Founder Counsel in writing (the "August 28 Letter"), which letter set forth the Special Committee’s proposal to recommend to the Board the authorization of Class C common stock and a substantially contemporaneous, unconditional dividend of one share of Class C common stock for approximately every nine shares of Class A common stock and Class B common stock, with any subsequent dividends subject to the Special Committee’s sole discretion. The August 28 Letter stated the Special Committee’s view that the proposal would enable the Company to raise equity capital at the scale required for its business plans and to use Class C common stock as employee compensation and acquisition consideration, in each case without diluting Mr. Zuckerberg’s voting interest, and that the dividend would establish a liquid market for Class C common stock of approximately $150 billion while affording Mr. Zuckerberg approximately $20 billion in immediate liquidity. On August 31, 2026, Founder Counsel had a call with Special Committee Counsel to preview Mr. Zuckerberg’s response to the August 28 Letter. Following the discussion, Founder Counsel delivered a letter response to Special Committee Counsel (the "August 31 Letter"). The August 31 Letter expressed Mr. Zuckerberg’s support for an unconditional dividend of Class C common stock to all shareholders, but conveyed Founder Counsel’s view that the proposed ratio of one share of Class C common stock for nine shares of Class A common stock and Class B common stock was too low and would create an unnecessary discount in the trading price of Class C common stock. The letter further stated that, while the Special Committee’s proposal appeared to sufficiently address the potential reduction in Mr. Zuckerberg’s voting power from sales of his equity to fund a tax under California Proposition 40, it did not otherwise address his liquidity concerns and that he would accordingly request a compensation arrangement commensurate with his contributions and market comparables, an outline of which Founder Counsel would provide at a meeting with the Special Committee later that week. The August 31 Letter further stated that resolution of these matters within two weeks was imperative in light of the Company’s financing needs, competitive pressures, and the timing of the vote on California Proposition 40. On September 1, 2026, the Special Committee held a regularly scheduled meeting with each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis. During the meeting, Special Committee Counsel reported on its conversations with Founder Counsel 26 surrounding the August 28 Letter and the August 31 Letter. Representatives of Moelis reviewed the basis for the dividend ratio set forth in the August 28 Letter, together with illustrative alternative ratios and the extent to which Mr. Zuckerberg could dispose of his economic interest under each while retaining his current voting power. They also reviewed alternative structures that could offer Mr. Zuckerberg liquidity while also minimizing the divergence between Mr. Zuckerberg’s economic ownership and his voting control, academic studies on the performance of controlled companies over time, the liquidity afforded to founders in precedent recapitalizations involving dividends of non-voting capital stock, and the discounts observed in precedent equity offerings by other companies. Among the alternative structures was a proposal for an exchange offer pursuant to which the Company would offer all shareholders the opportunity to exchange shares of Class A common stock or Class B common stock for Class C common stock at an exchange ratio in excess of 1:1 but to be within a limited range, noting that such a structure would afford Class A shareholders the choice to dilute their voting interest in exchange for a greater economic interest in the Company. Representatives of Moelis noted that such an exchange offer, in isolation, would have the result of further increasing Mr. Zuckerberg’s relative voting control. Following discussion, the Special Committee confirmed its view that the August 28 Letter represented the limit of what it could offer to Mr. Zuckerberg without concessions from Mr. Zuckerberg. The Special Committee also discussed what subsequent actions the Special Committee might take in the event that Mr. Zuckerberg were unwilling to provide meaningful concessions for the benefit of the holders of Class A common stock, including dissolution of the Special Committee or resignation from the Board. The Special Committee also reaffirmed its position that Mr. Zuckerberg’s compensation request remained a matter for the CN&G Committee. On September 2, 2026, the Special Committee held a discussion with Mr. Zuckerberg, Founder Counsel, Mr. Mahoney and Special Committee Counsel. Special Committee Counsel reviewed the terms of the August 28 Letter, noting that the dividend would be payable to all shareholders, would be unqualified, and would afford Mr. Zuckerberg approximately $20 billion of Class C common stock he could sell without affecting his existing voting interest. Mr. Zuckerberg responded that the dividend ratio was his principal concern as it would result in a significant discount of Class C common stock relative to the Class A common stock, in a subsequent equity raise, harming all shareholders. Mr. Zuckerberg also reiterated his liquidity needs in order to enable him to pay any tax imposed on him under California Proposition 40 and stated that he did not wish to go through additional protracted negotiations if taxes similar to California Proposition 40 were to be imposed in the future. The Special Committee conveyed that it understood Mr. Zuckerberg’s position and realized there could be a significant discount on Class C common stock at the dividend ratio proposed in the August 28 Letter, but that it could not approve a larger dividend unless Mr. Zuckerberg agreed to certain conditions that would address the Special Committee’s concerns that a larger dividend would allow Mr. Zuckerberg to continue to exercise voting control over the Company while allowing him to potentially sell a very substantial portion of his economic stake in the Company. Members of the Special Committee again proposed coupling any sale by Mr. Zuckerberg of Class C common stock with the sale of a proportionate number of shares of Class B common stock and certain sunset provisions as set forth in the Governance Proposal, and stated that they were open to other structures that addressed the same concern. On September 3, 2026, all members of the Special Committee held a meeting with each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis to discuss next steps in light of the prior day’s call with Mr. Zuckerberg. On September 5, 2026, Founder Counsel sent Special Committee Counsel a term sheet (the "September 5 Term Sheet") proposing the authorization of Class C common stock and a dividend of four shares of Class C common stock for each share of Class A common stock and Class B common stock. The September 5 Term Sheet incorporated the stapling concept the Special Committee had raised and proposed that Mr. Zuckerberg and his affiliates could not sell or otherwise transfer (including through hedging or pledging) any of the 80% of the Class C common stock he received in the dividend unless, for each such share of Class C common stock transferred, he concurrently transferred one share of Class B common stock to a third party (the "Stapling Condition"), with the remaining 20% not subject to the Stapling Condition. Five percent of the Class C common stock Mr. Zuckerberg received in the dividend would be released from the Stapling Condition each calendar quarter for sixteen consecutive quarters so long as he continued to serve as the Company’s Chief Executive Officer or otherwise held a position at the Company (so that all of it would be released after four years), and all shareholders would receive an additional dividend of one share of Class C common stock for each outstanding share of Class A common stock, Class B common stock and Class C common stock, not subject to the Stapling Condition, upon each doubling of the Company’s market capitalization. On September 6, 2026, all members of the Special Committee held a meeting with each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis to discuss the September 5 Term Sheet. Following discussion, the Special Committee reached a consensus to invite Mr. Zuckerberg and his representatives to present the September 5 Term Sheet and the proposals therein to the Special Committee. Later that same day, the Special Committee held a meeting with Mr. Zuckerberg, Founder Counsel, Mr. Mahoney and Special Committee Counsel, at which Mr. Zuckerberg explained the terms of the September 5 Term Sheet and his rationale for them. Mr. Zuckerberg stated his intention to remain with the Company for the foreseeable future but noted that, given the impending vote on California Proposition 40 and his personal circumstances, he would need to begin taking steps by mid-September in order to establish residency outside California by mid-October, and that he anticipated informing management of those steps immediately after the upcoming Board meeting that he would be taking steps to leave California. 27 On that same day, and following Mr. Zuckerberg’s and his counsel’s departure, the Special Committee reconvened with each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis. The Special Committee and its advisors discussed Mr. Zuckerberg’s stated timing and intention to begin taking the steps required to establish residency outside California. The Special Committee and its advisors further discussed the proposals set forth in the September 5 Term Sheet and the Special Committee’s proposed response. On September 7, 2026, all members of the Special Committee held a meeting with each of Special Committee Counsel and Moelis to discuss potential responses to the September 5 Term Sheet. Representatives of Moelis reviewed materials previously circulated to the Special Committee setting forth illustrative components of a counterproposal for a Potential Recapitalization, including a range of dividend ratios, together with various sale restrictions (including the Stapling Condition), sunset provisions, and other transfer restrictions. The Special Committee and its advisors discussed the relative merits of the alternatives, the interests of the current holders of Class A common stock, the circumstances in which a Potential Recapitalization might be submitted to a shareholder vote and the additional governance and protective provisions that would need to be associated with Class C common stock. At the conclusion of the meeting, the Special Committee determined to present a subset of the alternatives to Mr. Zuckerberg in person and to require in each alternative that Mr. Zuckerberg contractually agree to remain in California and to work from the Company’s headquarters given the value to the Company that he do so. On September 8, 2026, the Special Committee held a meeting with Mr. Zuckerberg, Founder Counsel and Mr. Mahoney, Special Committee Counsel, and Moelis. The Special Committee started the meeting by inviting representatives of Moelis to present materials previously shared with, and reviewed by, the Special Committee. Representatives of Moelis proceeded to present several illustrative alternatives for a Potential Recapitalization for discussion. Each option would afford Mr. Zuckerberg approximately $20 billion of immediate liquidity for any tax liability that may be imposed on him if California Proposition 40 became law, and each option was paired with certain sale and transfer restrictions on, or sunset provisions for, the Class B common stock tied to Mr. Zuckerberg ceasing to serve as the Company’s Chief Executive Officer or in a C-suite role. Mr. Zuckerberg expressed a willingness to agree to the stapling requirement, which would restrict his own sales of Class C common stock without restricting sales by any other shareholder, but responded that he would not agree to the additional proposed restrictions on the sale or transfer of his Class B common stock or to sunset provisions of that kind and asked for time to consider the structures. Members of the Special Committee then held further direct discussions with Mr. Zuckerberg over the course of the day, in which he confirmed that position. Following the meeting with Mr. Zuckerberg, the Special Committee determined it was advisable for its members to directly communicate and meet with Mr. Zuckerberg. Thereafter and over the course of the day, the members of the Special Committee held additional discussions with Mr. Zuckerberg with respect to the proposals presented to him earlier in the day. During these conversations, Mr. Zuckerberg made it clear he was willing to accept the Stapling Condition but opposed additional restrictions on the sale or transfer of his Class B common stock. Later that same day, the Special Committee held a second meeting with Mr. Zuckerberg, Mr. Mahoney and Special Committee Counsel, and Moelis. At the meeting, the Special Committee and Mr. Zuckerberg primarily discussed the mechanics of an exchange offer, one of the alternatives presented to Mr. Zuckerberg during the prior meeting that day. Representatives of Moelis discussed the benefits associated with the exchange offer, mainly the agency it would confer on the holders of Class A common stock with respect to their participation in the offer and the potential for a larger Class C common stock float, which would reduce any trading discount. Mr. Zuckerberg expressed concern about the inherent complexity and uncertainty involved in an exchange offer, its unprecedented nature and his skepticism that an exchange offer could be executed on his desired timeline and that the option did not adequately address his concern about his potential tax liabilities, including with respect to any tax that may be imposed on him by California Proposition 40. Separately, and following the meeting the next day, Special Committee Counsel outlined the potential terms of such an exchange offer to representatives of Nasdaq to seek comfort, on a no-names basis, that such an exchange offer would not violate Nasdaq Rule 5640, which prohibits public companies from disparately reducing or restricting the voting rights of existing shareholders through any corporate action or issuance and for which Nasdaq has issued interpretative guidance that generally, exchange offers with existing shareholders involving the issuance of voting stock for low-vote or no-vote stock are presumed to be prohibited under Nasdaq Rule 5640 as inherently coercive. Special Committee Counsel received no such preliminary comfort in the course of their discussion. On September 9, 2026, the Special Committee held a meeting attended by each of Moelis and Special Committee Counsel. During the meeting, the Special Committee and its advisors further discussed the structure for a Potential Recapitalization proposed in the September 5 Term Sheet and whether such structure could be modified to be economically equivalent to an exchange offer. The Special Committee and its advisors also discussed the merits of additional dividends linked to the Company’s stock price performance, with the Special Committee noting that such additional dividends could be beneficial to holders of Class A common stock given that the additional dividends would be conditioned on the Company’s continued extraordinary performance. Later that same day, the members of the Board, including the members of the Special Committee and Mr. Zuckerberg, had a regularly scheduled Board dinner. As had been previewed to Special Committee Counsel, during the dinner, Mr. 28 Zuckerberg updated the members of the Board in attendance on the status of negotiations regarding a Potential Recapitalization and that no agreement between him and the Special Committee had been reached with respect to a Potential Recapitalization. Mr. Zuckerberg further informed the present members of the Board of a number of steps he would be required to take to change his residency in light of the inclusion of California Proposition 40 on the ballot for the upcoming general election in California, and informed them that he planned to list his home in Palo Alto, California, for sale on September 13, 2026, which listing would become public information. Members of the Special Committee recused themselves from the dinner during Mr. Zuckerberg’s update and were not present during the update. On September 10, 2026, Founder Counsel delivered a revised term sheet to Special Committee Counsel (the "September 10 Term Sheet"). The September 10 Term Sheet reduced the proposed dividend to one share of Class C common stock for each share of Class A common stock and Class B common stock, from the twenty-for-one ratio Mr. Zuckerberg proposed on August 4 and the four-for-one ratio he proposed on September 5, and lengthened the release period from four years to eight. Eighty percent of the Class C common stock issued to Mr. Zuckerberg would be subject to the Stapling Condition, which was modified to apply only to sales and to permit one share of Class A or Class B common stock to be sold for each share of Class C common stock sold, giving him immediate liquidity with respect to the remaining 20%. The September 10 Term Sheet further provided that an additional 2.5% of the Class C common stock issued to Mr. Zuckerberg in the dividend would be released from the Stapling Condition for each quarter in which he served on the Company’s executive team or as executive chair of the Board (so that all of it, representing half of his economic stake in the Company, would be released after eight years). The September 10 Term Sheet further provided that, for fifteen years following the initial dividend, and so long as Mr. Zuckerberg so served, all shareholders would receive an additional dividend of one share of Class C common stock for each outstanding share of Class A common stock, Class B common stock, and Class C common stock upon each doubling of the Class C common stock share price. The September 10 Term Sheet described these performance-based terms as being in lieu of performance-based compensation that Mr. Zuckerberg would otherwise have been entitled to seek, without the Company incurring the significant cost of such a program. Finally, the September 10 Term Sheet stated Mr. Zuckerberg’s intention to remain a resident of California, or any other jurisdiction in which the Company maintains its corporate headquarters, for the duration of the agreement unless unforeseen circumstances made it prudent or advisable for him to reside elsewhere. On that same day, during a meeting with the independent members of the Board, the Special Committee provided an update on the status of a Potential Recapitalization. The Special Committee summarized the efforts its members and Mr. Zuckerberg had undertaken to negotiate a Potential Recapitalization that is in the best interests of the Company’s Class A shareholders while also addressing the Company’s financing objectives and Mr. Zuckerberg’s stated objectives. The Special Committee then informed the independent members of the Board that it felt it was progressing towards a mutually agreeable framework for a Potential Recapitalization, and that it would not be constructive for the Board to be involved in the negotiations at such time. No member of the Board provided any advice or direction to the members of the Special Committee with respect to a Potential Recapitalization. Following the Special Committee’s update on the status of a Potential Recapitalization, the Special Committee informed the present Board members that it believed Mr. Zuckerberg would proceed with establishing residency outside of California if agreement was not reached with respect to a Potential Recapitalization and that the Special Committee was moving expeditiously with that timing in mind. Later that day, following further discussion between the Special Committee and Mr. Zuckerberg regarding the September 10 Term Sheet, Founder Counsel sent Special Committee Counsel a revised term sheet (the "Revised September 10 Term Sheet") that Founder Counsel understood to memorialize that discussion and asked Special Committee Counsel to confirm. The Revised September 10 Term Sheet provided that the trigger for each additional dividend would be a thirty-day volume-weighted average trading price of the Class C common stock equal to double (or a further doubling of) the thirty-day volume-weighted average trading price of the Class A common stock immediately preceding September 10, 2026, provided that the internal rate of return on the Company’s stock price measured from that reference price was at least 10%, with no more than one such dividend for each doubling (each, a "Doubling Event"). The Revised September 10 Term Sheet replaced the fifteen-year limit on the additional dividends with the foregoing two conditions. It also provided that Mr. Zuckerberg’s existing compensation arrangement would remain unchanged. Later that day, Special Committee Counsel delivered a response (the “September 10 Response”) that, among other things, provided that any share of Class C common stock transferred not in compliance with the Stapling Condition would result in the automatic conversion of a proportional number of shares of Class B common stock into shares of Class A common stock; conditioned the quarterly releases and the additional dividends on both Mr. Zuckerberg’s full-time service on the executive team or as executive chair of the Board and his existing compensation arrangement remaining unchanged; measured each Doubling Event as of the date the definitive agreement was executed, rather than September 10, 2026; required that repurchases of Class C common stock equal or exceed repurchases of Class A common stock for the term of the agreement and that Class C common stock otherwise receive non-disparate economic treatment relative to the Class A common stock; recast Mr. Zuckerberg’s residency intention as a covenant to remain a resident of California, or any other jurisdiction in which the Company maintains its corporate headquarters, subject only to material unforeseen security circumstances; and provided that Mr. Zuckerberg’s existing compensation arrangement would remain unchanged for the term of the agreement. 29 On September 11, 2026, Founder Counsel delivered a further revised term sheet (the “September 11 Term Sheet”) substantially based on the September 10 Response. The September 11 Term Sheet confirmed that Mr. Zuckerberg withdrew his request for a performance-based compensation program and struck the requirement that his compensation remain unchanged as a condition to the quarterly releases and the additional dividends; permitted him to satisfy the Stapling Condition by converting one share of Class B common stock into Class A common stock for each share of Class C common stock sold, in lieu of a concurrent sale; revised the result of a non-compliant transfer of Class C common stock to the automatic conversion of an equal number of shares of Class B common stock into shares of Class A common stock; provided a thirty-day cure period for an inadvertent non-compliant sale or transfer; restored September 10, 2026 as the reference date for measuring each Doubling Event; and limited the residency provision to a term of eight years, recast as a statement of Mr. Zuckerberg’s intention rather than a commitment. On September 12, 2026, the Special Committee held a meeting with Special Committee Counsel and Moelis to discuss the September 11 Term Sheet and to prepare for a planned discussion with Mr. Zuckerberg later that day. During this meeting, representatives of Moelis reviewed their analysis of the economic terms of the September 11 Term Sheet, including the internal rates of return implied by numerous stock price doublings that would trigger additional dividends of Class C common stock and the implications of using a thirty-day volume-weighted average price measured as of September 10, 2026 as the reference measure for such thresholds. Representatives of Moelis also reviewed the Company’s recent stock price performance and illustrative analyses of the liquidity that would be available to Mr. Zuckerberg over various periods, both with and without such doublings, while retaining his current voting power and retaining at least 50% voting power. Later that same day, the Special Committee held a meeting with Mr. Zuckerberg, Founder Counsel and Special Committee Counsel. At the Special Committee’s request, Mr. Zuckerberg started the meeting by stating it was not his intention to ask for additional compensation, though he highlighted that circumstances may change in the ensuing years that would justify his need to revisit his compensation arrangement and he understood the proposal to prohibit him from even asking for different compensation arrangements. The Special Committee clarified that was not its intent and Mr. Zuckerberg could make a request for compensation to the CN&G Committee for its consideration in the future but also made it clear that it viewed the abandonment of his intent to make a compensation proposal as a critical part of the agreements set forth in the September 11 Term Sheet. Mr. Zuckerberg also clarified that it remained his intention to remain resident in California or any other jurisdiction that the Company is headquartered in and to continue serving on the Company’s executive team or as executive chair of the Board for the foreseeable future. However, in this era of rapid development, he also recognized that there may be unforeseen circumstances which would justify his need to relocate elsewhere and in such a case, it was his view that the agreements in the September 11 Term Sheet should not otherwise fall away. Between September 12 and September 13, 2026, the Special Committee held a number of additional meetings with Special Committee Counsel and Moelis. Special Committee Counsel and Founder Counsel held a number of additional discussions, and the Special Committee held one additional discussion with Mr. Zuckerberg to clarify certain discrete points in the September 11 Term Sheet, including that Mr. Zuckerberg’s ability to change his residency would be subject to only the occurrence of material unforeseen circumstances, and exchanged additional drafts of a term sheet. On September 14, 2026, the Special Committee and Mr. Zuckerberg agreed to a final term sheet providing for the agreements described in this Information Statement. The final term sheet states that its purpose is to create a publicly traded, non-voting security that the Company may use for capital raising, acquisitions, and employee compensation without diluting the voting power of the existing Class A common stock and Class B common stock; to establish a deep and liquid trading market for Class C common stock designed to minimize any trading discount; and to structure the creation of Class C common stock to include performance-based targets and full-time service requirements for Mr. Zuckerberg in lieu of a performance-based compensation program, without the Company incurring the cost of such a program.
